gress took up +h„ cau* 0 of the concerned workman. It 
has been stated in paragraph 4 of the workmen's written 
S*at°ment that the union rnlsM an industrial dispute 
against the arbitrary and illegal termination of service 
of the concerned vor v man before the Regional Labou” 
Commissioner tW^u^b * letter of the union dated 12lh 
April, 1971. said letter of l?lh April. 1971 appears 

to havo been "’ri'ten bv one P P. Singh, Secretary of 
the Khan Mazdr^- Congress, The opening s^ntencp of 
the letter is as Allows: “ T n ""cordance with the pro- 
visions of Industrial Disputes A°t 7947. wp am raising 
the fol T owing Industrial dispute". This letter does not 
refer to any resolution of the union authorising the 
S^rptarv to r*ig*> an industrial dispute before the Aslt. 
Labour Commissioner and the Regional Labour Com- 
mission 0 ^ The Secretary anneals to have acted -un 
mo tv without anv proper authority from the union, 
therefore, tber* is nothing on record to show that the 
nr«a«jont dispute ha« been raised hv the union on the 
basis of any resolution passed by the union. 

6. In as much as the locus-standi of the union has 

been challenged by the employer the onus lies on the 
union to establish its authority to raise an industrial 
dispute on behalf of the concerned workman. No such 
evidence has been adduced by the union before this 
Tribunal; in other words, the tin ion has failed to dis- 
charge the onus of proving that it was dulv authorised 
bv the workmen of the colliery to raise this dispute. 
That being the position the^ present dispute cannot be 
regarded as a collective dispute; at best It may be 
treated as no individual dispute unde^ section 2 A o* thp 
Industrial Disputes Act, The present dispute has been 
referred to this Tribunal as a collective dispute and has 
got tn be decided on that footing. From what has been 
stated abor* it is clear that the union is not entitled to 
represent the workman concerned, The present dispute 
therefor^ cannot br> entertained by this Tribunal. T^e 
reference is liable to on that ground. My 

conclusion on this point is based on the followin'* 
oases; (1) Workmen of D^arampaT Premrhand And 
Dharamoal Premchand. 1905 (T) L. L. J. 06B; NelM 
Cotton Mills And Labour Court, Madurai, 1905 (ID 
L.L.J, 96; ffl) Murueaona Mudaliar Rathine Mudaliar & 
Sons And Raju Mudaliar, 1965 (I) L.L.J. 489. 

7. Apart from what has been stated above, on merits 
also, in mv opinion, the union has got no case. In para- 
graph 2 of the written statement of the workmen it is 
simply stated that th° workman concerned wa* work- 
ing as Cap Lamo Maintenance Clerk in the colliery nf 
th em mover. Tt has not, been stated that he was 
erpnloved on o permanent basis. In paragraph 11 of +h" 
vmitten statement of the workmen it. has been stated 
thnt the workman concerned had beer working in the 
colllerv of the employer since 1968 as Can Lamp Main- 
tenance Clerk and that he was employed on a perma- 
nent basis. No evidence hag been produced to show 
that he had beep working in the colliery in question 
since 1968 , 


8. Ext. W9 series were filed by the union just to 
show that he was employed in the colliery in 1970. 
M.W, 1 the Manager of the colliery has however stated 
in his examination-in-chief that he does not know who 
has Issued the slips Ext. W9 series. The other slips 
filed by the union has not been exhibited at all 
and it is not denied that they relate to some other 
colliery. There is, therefore, nothing- on record to 
show that he was employed in the colliery prior to 
17th February, 1971. 

9. I have no reason 1*o disbelieve M.W, 1 who says 
in his examination in chief that he was employed on 
a temporary basis. The relevant portion of his de- 
position is as follows; “He (the concerned workman) 
worked in the colliery from 17th February, 1971 to 
19th March, 1971. I appointed this workman in place 
of permanent incumbent Shri Dibakar Mukherjee, 
Can Lamp Maintenance Clerk who went on leave from 
17th February, 1971 for one month. I did not give 
any appointment letter, I made it clear to him that 
this is a purely temporary job and I also told him 
that he would have to vacate when the permanent 
incumbent would come. The permanent incumbent 
Dibakar Mukherjee joined his duty on 20th March, 
1971. H°nce from that date the services of Shri Ram 
Kishun Prasad were terminated". There is no serious 
cross-examination on this point. 

10. The workman concerned in his deposition no 
doubt has said that he was appointed on a permanent 
basis but he has not been able to produce anv evidence 
in support of that statement. He has further stated in 
his examination -in-chief that he was not appointed in 
place of Shri Dibakar Mukherjee; but from Ext. Ml 
it is clear that bo was appointed in the leave vacancy 
of Dibakar Mukherjee, Ext. W4 does not show that 
the concerned workman was employed on a perma- 
nent basis. 

11. It is argued by Shri S. P. Singh that the con- 
cerned workman must be regarded as a permanent 
workman as he was employed in a post appertaining 
to one of the permanent operations of the colliery. I 
cannot accept this contention. Even a temporary 
workman can bo appointed for doing work of a per- 
manent nature. The nature of the work performed by 
the workman cannot indicate whether he is permanent 
or temporary. 

12. Shri Singh argues that no appointment can be 
made as is not provided for in the standing orders. 
Clause 3 of the standing orders classifies workmen 
into: (i) Permanent; (ii) Probationers: (ill) Badlis or 
Substitutes; (Iv) Temporarv and (v) Apprentices. 
The employer has not described the workman concer- 
ned as a badli. Shri Singh argues that th^ concerned 
workman is neither a probationer nor a badli nor a 
temporarv workman nor an Apprentice. He ig also 
not a temporary workman because according to the 
definition in clause 3 of the standing orders a tem- 
porary workman if? one who is engaged for a specified 
temporary work. Shri Singh argues that the concerned 
workman was not engaged for any temporarv work. 
According to Shri Singh as he is neither a probationer 
nor a badli nor temporary nor an apprentice he must 
b " ragarded as a permanent workman. This arument 
also I am unable to accept. The standing orders no 
where savs that a temporary appointment cannot he 
made in respect of a permanent work. If any work- 
man is appointed temporarily in respect of a permanent 
wo eV pnd bo accepts the appointment as a temporary 
workman, bo must b° regarded as a temporary work- 
man. Tn this connection reference may be made to 
t.bn derision of the Supreme Court in Rohtas Indus- 
trie? Ltd And Brijandan Pandev and other 1956 (II) 
I.LJR 444. In that case the company had a number 
of factories at Dalmianngar including a cement factory, 
power-house, pulp mil], paper factory, chemical fac- 
tory for the manufacture of certain acids and an 
asbestos cement factory. The company had a number 
of temporarv employees who were engaged tempo- 
rarily in connection with certain erection works for 



1874 


THIS GAZETTE OF INDIA: MAY 20, 1972/VAISAKHA 30, 1894 


[Part li- 


the extension and enlargement of those factories. The 
terms of employment of these employees were em- 
bodied in a temporary appointment from which was 
signed by the employees as well as the managemment. 
The work for which they were recruited was over some 
time before and after such work they were engaged 
in a permanent work in production department. The 
employer thereafter applied to the Labour Appellate 
Tribunal for permission to dismiss them. The permission 
was refused by the Tribunal but on aopeal was given 
by the Supreme Court for the following reason: 
“Under their terms of employment, temporary em- 
ploees could be moved from one work to the' other 
and the more circumstance that they were employed 
in a production department for some time, even if 
true, did not make them permanent employees; nor 
did the circumstance that they enjoyed some of the 
benefits of permanent employees make them perma~ 
nent”. On the same principles it may be said that the 
concerned workman employed on a temporary basis 
must be regarded as a temporary workman even though 
he was employed for the purpose of performing some 
permanent work in the colliery. 

13. It is therefore clear from what has been stated 
above that the action of the management of East 
Lohapati ColUery in terminating the services of Shri 
Ham Kishun Prasad, Cap Lamn Maintenance Clerk with 
effect from 19th March, 1971 is justified and, therefore, 
he is not entitled to any relief. My award is as 
follows: As the present dispute has not been raised by 
a substantial number of workmen working in the 
colliery it cannot be regarded as a collective dispute 
and it cannot be decided as such, In other words no 
award can be given as there is no dispute. My further 
award is that even on merits the action of the 
management in terminating the services of Shri Ham 
Kishun Prasad is justified and that he is not entitled 
to any relief. 

(Sd.) A. C, Sen, 
Presiding Officer.

DEPARTMENT OF COMPANY AFFAIRS 

Cost and Works Accountants 
New Delhi, the 8th May 1972 

S.O* 1242. — In pursuance of clause (lii) of sub-section 
<1) of section 4 of the Cost and Works Accountants 
Act, 1959 (23 of 1959), the Central Government hereby 
specifies the following conditions which a person who, 
at the commencement of the said Act was engaged in 
the practice of cost accountancy in India, shall fulfil 
to be entitled to have his name entered in the Register, 
namely: — 

1. He must have passed the Intermediate Examina- 

tion of a recognised University or an equivalent 
examination. 

2. He must have been mainly In continuous prac- 

tice as a Cost Accountant for a period of five 
years prior to the 28th May, 1959. 

3. He must have had as clients five concerns on an 

average per annum during two years im- 
mediately preceding the date of his application 
and in judging the volume of his practice, each 
of five concerns should have had as fixed assets 
of the aggregate value of not less than rupees 
fifteen lakhs and should have employed not 
less than 100 workers in that concern. 

Explanation. — The word “concern” shall for the pur- 
poses of this notification mean a concern engaged in 
production, processing, manufacturing or mining activi- 
ties. 

[No. 10/27/70-IGC.] 
M. C. VARMA, Under Secy.
